# Arabic course review sheet

Generated from the implementation by `docs/arabic-course-review/build-review-package.mjs`. **Every item below is UNREVIEWED.** Nothing here has been approved by a qualified Arabic teacher.
Read `REVIEWER-GUIDE.md` first. Record decisions in `review-items.csv` (same item IDs) or tick the boxes here.

Decision key: ☐ approved · ☐ correction needed · ☐ uncertain. “Priority” is a machine-assigned suggestion (see the guide), not a linguistic judgement. Arabic text is shown exactly as implemented; open this file in a viewer with an Arabic font.

| H-MARK-DAMMA | harakat<br>mark explanation + example | ضمة  \|  mark: ُ  \|  example: بُ  \|  word: كُتُب | كتب  /  bu  \|  kutub | Damma: short “u”. A small loop-like mark ABOVE the letter. It adds a short “u” as in “put”.  Example word meaning: books |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |

## Sukun, shadda and tanween

| ID | Category | Arabic (as implemented) | Plain / transliteration | English / explanation / notes | Flags | Priority | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| H-MARK-SUKUN | harakat<br>mark explanation + example | سكون  \|  mark: ْ  \|  example: بْ  \|  word: مَكْتَب | مكتب  /  b  \|  maktab | Sukun: no vowel. A small circle above the letter. The consonant is pronounced with NO vowel after it, joining directly to the next sound.  Example word meaning: office, desk | sukun | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H-MARK-SHADDA | harakat<br>mark explanation + example | شدّة  \|  mark: ّ  \|  example: بَّ  \|  word: مُعَلِّم | معلم  /  bba  \|  muʿallim | Shadda: doubled consonant. A small w-like mark above the letter. The consonant is said twice (held longer). When a vowel mark is also present, it sits above or below the shadda.  Example word meaning: teacher (male) | shadda | high (ordering of shadda and vowel marks) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H-MARK-TANWEEN | harakat<br>mark explanation + example | تنوين  \|  mark: ًٌٍ  \|  example: كِتَابٌ  \|  word: كِتَابًا | كتابا  /  kitaabun  \|  kitaaban | Tanween: -an, -in, -un. A doubled vowel mark at the END of an indefinite noun, adding an “n”: ـً (-an), ـٍ (-in), ـٌ (-un). Fathatayn usually sits on an extra alif: ـًا. In everyday speech the final -n is often dropped at the end of a sentence.  Example word meaning: a book | tanween; non-joining-letter-inside-word | high (describes pausal-form behaviour and the alif seat for fathatayn) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H2-INTRO | harakat<br>lesson introduction |  |  | Three more marks finish the set: sukun means “no vowel here”, shadda doubles a consonant, and tanween adds a final -an, -in or -un to indefinite nouns. |  | high (factual claims about when marks are written) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H2-Q1 | quick-check<br>question + answer options |  |  | Question: What does sukun (ـْ) tell you?  Options: Add a short a / [CORRECT] No vowel after this consonant / Double the consonant / Add -un  Explanation shown: Sukun marks a consonant with no vowel, as in مَكْتَب (mak-tab). | sukun | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H2-Q2 | quick-check<br>question + answer options |  |  | Question: What does shadda (ـّ) do?  Options: [CORRECT] Doubles the consonant / Removes the consonant / Adds -an / Makes a long vowel  Explanation shown: Shadda means the consonant is doubled, as in مُعَلِّم (mu-ʿal-lim). | shadda | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| H2-Q3 | quick-check<br>question + answer options |  |  | Question: Which tanween ending is ـٌ ?  Options: -an / -in / [CORRECT] -un / -aa  Explanation shown: Dammatayn (ـٌ) gives -un, as in كِتَابٌ (kitaabun). | tanween | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |

## Sentence pattern: هَٰذَا + masculine noun

| ID | Category | Arabic (as implemented) | Plain / transliteration | English / explanation / notes | Flags | Priority | Decision |

| PAT-THIS-M | sentence-pattern<br>pattern title and explanation shown to learners | هَٰذَا + noun |  | هَٰذَا + noun: “This is a …” (masculine). Use هَٰذَا (hādhā, “this”) before a masculine noun to say “This is a …”. Arabic has no word for “is” here: the sentence is just هَٰذَا followed by the noun. The noun usually ends in -un (ـٌ) when it means “a …”. | dagger-alif; tanween; non-joining-letter-inside-word | high (grammar explanation (gender agreement, endings, definiteness)) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |

## Page text

| ID | Category | Arabic (as implemented) | Plain / transliteration | English / explanation / notes | Flags | Priority | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P-LEAD | page-text<br>hero sentence |  |  | An interactive beginner course in Modern Standard Arabic: learn all 28 letters and how they connect, the short-vowel marks, vocabulary by topic and simple sentences, with a daily spaced-repetition review and typing practice. Free, no account, and your progress stays on your device. |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO1 | page-text<br>heading |  |  | What you will learn |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO2 | page-text<br>paragraph |  |  | The course follows a clear beginner path: 1. the 28 letters, their names, sounds and shapes; 2. harakat (fatha, kasra, damma, sukun, shadda, tanween); 3. reading and typing from single letters up to short sentences, using the on-screen Arabic keyboard; 4. starter vocabulary (numbers, colours, family, greetings) and everyday vocabulary (food, home, daily routines, clothing, time, places, travel, shopping, weather, school and work); 5. sentence patterns such as “This is a …” and “Where is …?”; 6. eight practical conversations; 7. reading comprehension, from short sentences to beginner stories. Each letter lesson ends with a short check, and a lesson is marked complete only when you pass it. |  | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO3 | page-text<br>heading |  |  | How Arabic letters connect |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO4 | page-text<br>paragraph | ا د ذ ر ز و  \|  دار  |  | Arabic is written right to left, and most letters join to the next one, changing shape by position: alone, at the beginning, in the middle, or at the end of a connected group. Six letters never join to the letter after them: ا د ذ ر ز و. After one of these the next letter starts a fresh shape, which is why words such as دار (daar, “house”) look broken into pieces. | non-joining-letter | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO5 | page-text<br>heading |  |  | Your learning path |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO6 | page-text<br>paragraph |  |  | The dashboard shows where you are on the path and what to do next. A step is marked complete only when you have really passed it, and nothing is locked, so you can skip ahead whenever you like. |  | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO7 | page-text<br>heading |  |  | Daily review |  | standard | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO8 | page-text<br>paragraph |  |  | Each time you answer a question about a letter, a word or a sentence, the course records the result for that item and schedules its next review. A correct answer moves the item to a longer interval; a miss brings it back tomorrow. Open the daily review to practise whatever is due. Your progress and review schedule are saved on your device only, with no account. |  | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO9 | page-text<br>paragraph |  |  | Beta. The Arabic content of this course has not yet been reviewed by a qualified Arabic teacher; a review is planned. If you spot a mistake, please tell us on the contact page. |  | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
| P-INTRO10 | page-text<br>paragraph |  |  | Audio uses your device’s text-to-speech voice where available. It approximates pronunciation but is not a native recording, and transliteration is only a rough guide. Examples are Modern Standard Arabic; dialects pronounce some letters differently. |  | high (explains joining rules and dialect scope to learners) | ☐ ☐ ☐ |
